(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k))
around
c_{0}
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

iii)

A flat handle transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k))
around
c_{0}
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

iv)

A rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(G-A,\Omega)
in
\Delta
with breadth
b\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)-1]
and depth at most
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)
,
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)
, and an extended
k
-surface-wall
D
with signature
(0,0,b)
, such that
D
is grounded in
\rho^{\prime}
, the base cycles of
D
are the cycles
C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)-1-k},\dots,C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)-1}
, and there exists a bijection between the vortices
v
of
\rho^{\prime}
and the vortex segments
S_{v}
of
D
, where
v
is the unique vortex contained in the disk
\Delta_{S_{v}}
defined by the trace of the inner cycle of the nest of
S_{v}
, and
\Delta_{S_{v}}
is chosen to avoid the trace of the simple cycle of
D
.

In particular, the set
A
, the
K_{t}
-minor model, the transaction
\mathcal{P}
, the rendition
\rho^{\prime}
, and the extended surface-wall
D
can each be found in time
\mathbf{poly}(t+s+p+k)|E(G)||V(G)|^{2}
.

Using our existing tools, we can almost get to
??
with ease.
In particular, the first three options of
??
can immediately be refined using our existing tools to yield one of the options in our theorem.
To deal with the last option, we will have to do a bit more work and import, as well as modify several results from
[
42
]
.

Orthogonalisation.

We first prove a variant of the following central lemma within
[
42
]
, which allows us to find a transaction that is orthogonal to all cycles except the innermost one of the nest.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
s(p-1)+1
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1},X_{2}
.

Then there exists an exposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{2},C_{3},\dots,C_{s}\}
,

ii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
connects vertices of
X_{1}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
to vertices of
X_{2}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
, and

iii)

the intersection of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
is fully contained in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Whilst this lemma is quite useful, for our application we cannot afford to lose a cycle of the nest each time we apply it.
Luckily, we will be using it in a context in which we have more structure.
In our setting the transaction together with the nest has a vortex-free rendition.
Here we show that we can find a transaction that is orthogonal to the entirety of the nest.
We state this lemma without reference to our special type of decompositions, as it may find applications outside of this work.
A similar result is presented in
[
79
]
(see Lemma 4.16).

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
, and let
H
be the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an unexposed transaction of order
s(p+1)+1
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1},X_{2}
, such that there exists a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(H\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P},\Omega)
that agrees with
\rho
on
H
.

Then there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and unexposed in
\rho
,

ii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
connects vertices of
X_{1}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
to vertices of
X_{2}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
, and

iii)

the intersection of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
is fully contained in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Unsurprisingly, we start by applying
??
to
\mathcal{P}
, which yields a transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p^{\prime}\coloneqq p+2
.
We note that due to the existence of
\rho^{\prime}
both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
must be planar.
Thus, we may assume that
\mathcal{Q}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}\}
is indexed naturally.

According to
??
,
\mathcal{Q}
is already orthogonal to all cycles in
\mathcal{C}
except for
C_{1}
.
Thus our goal will be the rectify this last non-orthogonal part of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Furthermore, since the intersection of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}
with the inner graph
H^{\prime}
of
C_{1}
is found entirely in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
, the rendition
\rho^{\prime}
in particular tells us that there is a vortex-free rendition of
(H\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q},\Omega)
, which can be derived from
\rho^{\prime}
.
For the sake of simplicity we will directly work with
\rho^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is cozy and
\mathcal{Q}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C_{1}\}
, we also note that there cannot exist a
C_{1}
-path in
H\cap\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
, as this path would have to be disjoint from
C_{2}
and stick out away from
c_{0}
in
\rho
.

We let
a_{i}\in X_{1}
and
b_{i}\in X_{2}
be the endpoints of
P_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}
for all
i\in[p^{\prime}]
.
For both
j\in[2]
, let
\mathcal{R}_{j}
be the set of
X_{j}
-
C_{1}
-paths in
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
, which contains exactly
p^{\prime}
paths.
For all
i\in[p^{\prime}]
, we let
a_{i}^{\prime}
be the second endpoint of the path in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
that has
a_{i}
as an endpoint and define
b_{i}^{\prime}
analogously.

Let
i\in[p^{\prime}]
and let
P
be a non-trivial
P_{i}
-path in
C_{1}
.
Suppose that
a_{j}^{\prime}
is found in
V(P)
for some
j\in[p^{\prime}]\setminus\{i\}
, but
b_{j}^{\prime}
is not.
Then
a_{j}^{\prime}
cannot be an endpoint of
P
and is thus an internal vertex of
P
.
Due to the existence of the vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
and the coziness of
\mathcal{C}
, there also cannot exist any non-trivial
C_{1}
-paths in
P_{j}\cap H
.
Thus
P_{j}
must somehow reach
b_{j}^{\prime}
starting from
a_{j}^{\prime}
via a subpath of
P_{j}
found entirely in
H^{\prime}
.
This is clearly impossible if
b_{j}^{\prime}
is not found in
V(P)
.

As a consequence, for all distinct
i,j\in[p^{\prime}]
, there exists a unique
P_{i}
-path in
C_{1}
that contains both
a_{j}^{\prime}
and
b_{j}^{\prime}
.
Let
i\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
.
Due to the fact that
\rho^{\prime}
is a vortex-free rendition, the trace of
P_{i}
separates the traces of
P_{i-1}
and
P_{i+1}
on
\Delta
.
Thus we further know that
a_{i-1}^{\prime}
and
a_{i+1}^{\prime}
are found in distinct
P_{i}
-paths of
C_{1}
.
This in particular implies that for each
i\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
there exist exactly two
P_{i}
-paths in
C_{1}
that contain vertices of other paths from
\mathcal{Q}
, one which contains vertices of paths with a lower index than
i
and the second containing vertices of paths with a higher index than
i
.
Furthermore, each of these two paths contains vertices from both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.

Note that for
P_{1}
and
P_{p^{\prime}}
there may not exist a non-trivial
C_{1}
-path in
G
.
Let
S_{1},S_{2}
be the two unique
P_{1}
-
P_{p^{\prime}}
-paths in
C_{1}
.
We choose these indices such that
a_{i}^{\prime}\in S_{1}
and
b_{i}^{\prime}\in S_{2}
for all
i\in[2,p^{\prime}]
.
For all paths
P_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}
, with
i\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
, there exists an
S_{1}
-
S_{2}
-path
Q_{i}
in
P_{i}\cap H^{\prime}
.
Let
a_{i}^{\prime\prime}
be the endpoint of
Q_{i}
in
S_{1}
and let
b_{i}^{\prime\prime}
be the endpoint of
Q_{i}
in
S_{2}
.

For each
i\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
we let
S_{1}^{i}
be the unique
a_{i}^{\prime}
-
a_{i}^{\prime\prime}
-path in
S_{1}
and let
S_{2}^{i}
be the unique
b_{i}^{\prime}
-
b_{i}^{\prime\prime}
-path in
S_{2}
.
We claim that for both
j\in[2]
, the path
S_{j}^{i}
is disjoint from
\bigcup(\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{P_{i}\})
.
Suppose this is not the case, then
S_{j}^{i}
contains at least one of the two
P_{i}
-paths, say
P^{\prime}
, in
C_{1}
that contain vertices of other paths from
\mathcal{Q}
.
Note that
S_{j}^{i}
is disjoint from
X_{3-j}
and thus
P^{\prime}
is disjoint from
X_{3-j}
, which contradicts that
P^{\prime}
must contain vertices of both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
Thus our claim holds.

We now construct
p^{\prime}-2=p
paths
Q_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,Q_{p^{\prime}-1}^{\prime}
as follows.
For each
i\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
start at
a_{i}
and follow
P_{i}
until
a_{i}^{\prime}
.
From
a_{i}^{\prime}
walk towards
a_{i}^{\prime\prime}
using
S_{1}^{i}
.
Then walk towards
b_{i}^{\prime\prime}
using
Q_{i}
, reach
b_{i}^{\prime}
via
S_{2}^{i}
and reach
b_{i}
via
P_{i}
.

The paths
Q_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,Q_{p^{\prime}-1}^{\prime}
we can construct this way are pairwise disjoint, orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
, exposed in
\rho
, and the intersection of
\bigcup\{Q_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,Q_{p^{\prime}-1}^{\prime}\}
with
H^{\prime}
is fully contained in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
by construction.
This completes our proof.
∎

We will also require the ability to manipulate radial linkages.
In particular, we will need to make a radial linkage orthogonal in certain contexts.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, let
\rho
be a
\Sigma
-rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
, and let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be two linkages of the same order.
If for each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
there exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
with the same endpoints as
Q
, we say that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are
end-identical
.

Proposition 7.7

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
42
]
)
.

Let
s,r
with
r\leq s
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
\Sigma
-rendition
\rho
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm running in
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
-time that finds
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Finding specific (odd) minors in extended surface walls.

Our ultimate goal is to prove by induction that we will at some point have to arrive in one of the options of
??
if we try to refine the kind of society mentioned in the setting of the theorem.
For this purpose we need one last tool which allows us to say that the iterative process that we use will have to terminate after finding too many disjoint vortices.

For this purpose we first import a lemma that builds a large
K_{t}
-minor model controlled by the base wall of an extended surface wall
W
if
W
has enough vortices hosting crosses.

Proposition 7.8

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
42
]
)
.

Let
t
be a positive integer,
\Sigma
be the sphere, and let
W
be an extended
(2t+8)
-surface-wall with
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)
vortices.
Moreover, let
G
be the union of
W
and
(t-3)(t-4)
vertex-disjoint
V(W)
-paths
L_{i},R_{i}
, with
i\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)]
, such that for every
i\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)]
, the paths
L_{i}
and
R_{i}
form a cross on the innermost cycle of the nest of the
i
th vortex-segment of
W
, both
L_{i}
and
R_{i}
are internally disjoint from
W
, and the endpoints of
L_{i}
and
R_{i}
are endpoints of rails of the
i
th vortex-segment of
W
.
Then
G
contains a
K_{t}
-minor controlled by
W
.

Moreover, such a minor model of
K_{t}
can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(|V(G)|)
.

We will need a modified version of this that allows us to find
\mathscr{U}_{t}
as an odd minor instead.
Luckily this is not too difficult to prove.
Given an even cycle
C
and a path
P
, that is internally disjoint from
C
but has both endpoints in
V(C)
, we call
P

parity breaking
if
C\cup P
does not have a proper 2-colouring.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
h
be a positive integer,
\Sigma
be the sphere, and let
W
be an parity
(2h+1)
-surface-wall with signature
(h^{o},h^{e},c^{o},c^{e},b^{o},b^{e})
such that
b^{e}\geq h
.
Moreover, let
G
be the union of
W
and
h
vertex-disjoint
V(W)
-paths
P_{i}
, with
i\in[h]
, such that for every
i\in[h]
, the endpoints of
P_{i}
are on the innermost cycle
C_{1}^{i}
of the nest of the
i
th even vortex-segment of
W
and
P_{i}
is parity breaking for
C_{1}^{i}
.
Then
G
contains an odd
\mathscr{U}_{h}
-minor model controlled by
W
.

Moreover, an odd minor model of
\mathscr{U}_{h}
can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(|V(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
S_{0},S_{1},\cdots,S_{h}
be the segments in
W
, where
S_{0}
is a
(2h+1)
-wall segment while
S_{1},\cdots,S_{h}
are even
(2h+1)
-vortex segments.
We find the odd minor model of a universal parity breaking grid of order
h
as follows:
14
14

14

In our construction we will consider the
\mathscr{U}_{h}
as the
90
-degree rotation of what is depicted in
??
so that the red edges are in horizontal paths.

Let
C_{1},\cdots,C_{2h+1}
be the base cycles of
W
in order such that
C_{2h+1}
is the simple cycle of
D
.
We will find vertex-disjoint paths from the
i
-th left boundary to the
i
-th right boundary of
S_{0}
, each disjoint from
S_{0}
, such that odd-numbered paths have the same parity with the paths with the same endpoints in
S_{0}
, while the even-numbered paths have the opposite parity.
Then the resulting graph contains
\mathscr{U}_{h}
as an odd minor.

Now we explain how to find paths with the desired parity.
We will find
2h+1
vertex-disjoint paths
Q_{1}^{i},Q_{2}^{i},\cdots,Q_{2h+1}^{i}
in
S_{i}
, where the endpoints of
Q_{j}^{i}
are in the
j
-th left and right boundary of
S_{i}
, that have the same parity as path with same endpoints in the base wall, except for
Q_{2i}^{i}
, which has the opposite parity.

For each
Q_{j}^{i}
, with
j\in[2i-1]
, we start from
j
-th left boundary of
S_{i}
and follow along
C_{i}
until we have met the
j
-th path of the rails of
S_{i}
.
We then follow along this rail until we have met the outermost
j
-th cycle of the nest of
S_{j}
.
From here we travel “around” the vortex following the nest of
S_{j}
, by choosing the direction that lets us encounter the last rail before we would encounter the
(2h)
-th rail.
However, before that happens, when first meeting the
(8h+5-j)
-th rail, we go back through that rail to
C_{i}
and follow it to arrive at the

-th right boundary of
S_{i}
.

For each
Q_{j}^{i}
, with
j\in[2i+1,2h+1]
, we simply follow along
C_{i}
from the left to the right boundary of
S_{i}
.
Since
S_{i}
is an even-vortex segment, each
P_{j}^{i}
with
j\neq 2i
has desired parity.

Now we define
Q_{2i}^{i}
.
We start from
j
-th left boundary of
S_{i}
and follow along
C_{i}
until we have met the
(2h+1)
-th path of the rails of
S_{i}
.
Next we follow along this rail to the end until we have met the innermost cycle
C_{1}^{i}
.
Let
x_{1},x_{2}
be the endpoints of
P_{i}
, and for
k\in[2]
, let
y_{k}
be the endpoint of
(2h+k)
-th path of the rail.
(We may assume that each rail meets with

in unique vertex.)
Since
C_{1}^{i}
is

-connected, there exists an

-

-linkage in

.
We move our growing path

through one of the paths in this linkage, then follow
P_{i}
, and come back to the endpoint of
(2h+2)
-th path of the rails.
Next we run down through this rail to go back to
C_{i}
, and then follow it to arrive at the
(2i)
-th right boundary of
S_{i}
.

Note that since
P_{i}
is a parity breaking path for
C_{1}^{i}
, the path
Q_{2i}^{i}
has the opposite parity compared with a path that has same endpoints in the base wall.

Now for each
j\in[2h+1]
, by concatenating the
Q_{j}^{i}
using the segments of
C_{j}
, we obtain the desired path between
j
-th left and right boundary of
S_{0}
.∎

Finding an unexposed even-faced planar transaction.

Our first task is to find an unexposed even-faced planar transaction to refine the given even-faced rendition. This can be done easily thanks to
??
and
??
after we find an unexposed transaction in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
s,p,d
be positive integers and
b
be a non-negative integer. Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with an even-faced rendition
\rho
in the disk
\Delta
with
b
vortices
c_{1},\ldots,c_{b}
of depth at most
d
and a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
and let
H
be the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
p^{\prime}\coloneqq(b+1)p+bd
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. Then there exists an unexposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that there is an even-faced vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(H\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Omega)
that agrees with
\rho
on
H
.

In particular, there exists an algorithm that, when given
\rho
,
(G,\Omega)
, and
\mathcal{P}
as input, finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

We first show that for each vortex
c
, the number of paths in
\mathcal{P}
that meets
c
as the first vortex is bounded.
Let
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{d^{\prime}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be the collection of such paths, and let
X_{1}^{\prime}
be the end segment of
\mathcal{Q}
in
X_{1}
.
Also let
(\sigma(c),\Omega_{c})
be the vortex society of
c
.
Suppose that
d^{\prime}\coloneqq|\mathcal{Q}|>d
.
For each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
, let
v_{i}
be the first vertex in
Q_{i}
that meets
c
, and
Q_{i}^{\prime}
be the subpath of
Q_{i}
between
v_{i}
and
X_{1}^{\prime}
.
Let
Y_{1}
be the segment of
\Omega_{c}
between
v_{1}
and
v_{d^{\prime}}
such that the disk
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta
bounded by
Q_{1}^{\prime},Q_{d^{\prime}}^{\prime},X_{1}^{\prime}
, and
Y_{1}
does not contain the vortex
c
.
Note that
Y_{1}
contains all
v_{i}
’s.
Let
Y_{2}=\Omega_{c}\setminus Y_{1}
.
Since
Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{d^{\prime}}^{\prime}
meet no other vortex and cannot be crossed by other paths, each path in
\mathcal{Q}
must pass through
Y_{2}
to arrive at
X_{2}
.
Let
w_{i}
be the first vertex in
Q_{i}
that meets
Y_{2}
, and let
u_{i}
be the last vertex in
Q_{i}
that meets with
Y_{1}
before
w_{i}
.
Then this gives
d^{\prime}
disjoint
Y_{1}
-
Y_{2}
-paths in
c
.
Since
c
has depth at most
d
, we have
d^{\prime}\leq d
, a contradiction to the bound on the depth of
c
.

Therefore, at most
bd
paths in
\mathcal{P}
can meet a vortex.
Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be the paths that do not meet a vortex.
Now let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{b+1}^{\prime}
be disjoint collections of
p
consecutive paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
.
We have
b
vortices and each vortex can be contained in at most one strip society of
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
, so there is an
i\in[b+1]
such that the strip society of
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
does not contain a vortex.
Then
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
is the desired unexposed planar transaction of order at least
p
.
Furthermore, by construction, there exists an even-faced vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(H\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Omega)
that agrees with
\rho
on
H
.
∎

For the next lemma, we define a function
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lem:separating_evenfaced_planar_transaction}}:\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lem:separating_evenfaced_planar_transaction}}(s,b,p,h,d)\coloneqq 2((b+1)s(2ph+bp+p+1)+bd+b+1).

We now further refine the unexposed transaction found in

, yielding one of our desired odd minors, a separating even-faced planar transaction, or a separating odd handle.

Finally, we will need the following simple rerouting argument that allows us to attach a grounded linkage to a radial linkage.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
s,p,d,k
be positive integers with
s\geq k
. Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
\Delta
-rendition
\rho
in the disk
\Delta
, a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
, and
\mathcal{R}
be a radial linkage for
\mathcal{C}
of order
k
with
B\subseteq V(\Omega)
being the set of endpoints of the paths of
\mathcal{R}
in
V(\Omega)
.
Further, let
H
be the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
, and let

be a set of size

.

If there is an
A
-

-linkage

of order

in

of size

-

-linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order

in
G
such that
V(\mathcal{P})\cap V(H)\subseteq V(\mathcal{L})
.
Moreover, there is an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(k|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
be a set of size
k
.
Suppose that there is no
A^{\prime}
-
B
-linkage of order
k
in
G
.
We may assume that each edge of

is contained in one of

,
V(\mathcal{L})
, or
V(\mathcal{R})
.
Then by
??
, there exists a separator

of size at most

between
A^{\prime}
and
B
.
Since
|S|\leq k-1
, there is some

such that

.
There are
k
paths between

and

, which are subpaths of
\mathcal{L}
, so

and some path in

intersect in

As there are also

and

\mathcal{R}
, we can also connect

to

, which is a contradiction to

-

-separator.
Therefore, there is an

-

-linkage

of order

in

.
Furthermore, there is an algorithm that finds

in time

We next introduce several notions that we will use to prove our main theorem.

Society configurations.

Let
s,k
be positive integers.
We call the tuple
\mathfrak{S}=((G,\Omega),\rho,c,\Delta,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{L},X,Y)
an
(s,k)
-society configuration
if

i)

(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c
in the disk
\Delta
with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
,

ii)

\mathcal{L}
is a radial linkage of order
k
,

iii)

X
is the set of endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
in
C_{1}
, and

iv)

Y
is the set of endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
in
\Omega
.

Moved in society configurations.

Let
\mathfrak{S}=((G,\Omega),\rho,c,\Delta,\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},\mathcal{L},X,Y)
be an
(s,k)
-society configuration.
We define an
(s-m-1,k)
-society configuration
\mathfrak{S}^{-m}=((G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime},c,\Delta^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
that is obtained by
moving in by
m
by removing the
m+1
outermost cycles of the nest
\mathcal{C}
as follows:
Let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{s-m}
-society in
\rho
and
\Delta^{\prime}
be the disk bounded by the trace of
C_{s-m}
.
Furthermore, we let
\rho^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\rho
to
G^{\prime}
, let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s-m-1}\}
, and let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
be the set of
\Omega^{\prime}
-
V(C_{1})
subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{L}
.
Also, let
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
be the set of endpoints of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
in
C_{1}
and
\Omega^{\prime}
, respectively.
Note that there are
m
cycles
C_{s-m+1},\ldots,C_{s}
that are disjoint with
G^{\prime}
.

Dividing a society configuration into two parts

In the course of the proof of the society classification theorem, we repeatedly divide a society configuration into two parts.
Let
\mathfrak{S}=((G,\Omega),\rho,c,\Delta,\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},\mathcal{L},X,Y)
be an
(s,k)
-society configuration.
Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p}\}
be a separating even-faced planar transaction of order
p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
with
(G_{\mathcal{P}},\Omega_{\mathcal{P}})
being the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society, such that the restriction of
\rho
to
(G-[\sigma_{\rho}(c)])\cup G_{\mathcal{P}}
is even-faced.
We
divide the
(s,k)
-society configuration
\mathfrak{S}
by the transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p=2k+2s+2
into two smaller
(s,k)
-society configurations
\mathfrak{C}^{L}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{R}
as follows.

(G,\Omega)
\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\mathcal{P}}
\color[rgb]{0,0.9,0.9}{(G^{L},\Omega^{L})}
\color[rgb]{0,0.9,0.9}{(G^{R},\Omega^{R})}
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathcal{L}^{L}}
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathcal{L}^{R}}

:
An illustration of dividing a society configuration into two parts:
We start from a society
(G,\Omega)

First we define four subtransactions
\mathcal{P}^{L}
,
\mathcal{P}^{R}
,
\mathcal{P}^{L,M}
,
\mathcal{P}^{R,M}
of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
in such a way that there are
k
paths in
\mathcal{P}^{L}
and
\mathcal{P}^{R}
taken from the left-most
k
and the right-most
k
paths of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. Moreover, the remaining paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
are split into two so that there are
s+1
paths in
\mathcal{P}^{L,M}=\{P_{1}^{L},\ldots,P_{s+1}^{L}\}
and in
\mathcal{P}^{R,M}=\{P_{1}^{R},\ldots,P_{s+1}^{R}\}
, the former indexed naturally and the latter indexed backwards with respect to the indices of paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{L}=

\displaystyle\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{L,M}=

\displaystyle\{P_{k+1},\ldots,P_{k+s+1}\},\text{ where we let }P_{a}^{L}\coloneqq P_{k+a}\text{ for }a\in[s+1],

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{R,M}=

\displaystyle\{P_{k+s+2},\ldots,P_{k+2s+2}\},\text{ where we let }P_{a}^{R}\coloneqq P_{k+2s+3-a}\text{ for }a\in[s+1],\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{R}=

\displaystyle\{P_{k+2s+3},\ldots,P_{2k+2s+2}\}.

•

Let
m\in\{L,R\}
.
For
l\in[s]
, let
u_{l}^{m},v_{l}^{m}
be the vertices of
P_{l}^{m}
in
C_{l}
such that the
(u_{l}^{m},v_{l}^{m})
-path does not contain any vertices in
C_{l}
other than its endpoints. Let
Q_{l}^{m}
be the path in
C_{l}
with
u_{l}^{m}
and
v_{l}^{m}
being its endpoints such that it does not intersect
P_{l+1}^{m}
.
Further, let
C_{l}^{m}
be the cycle defined by the union of two paths
u_{l}^{m}P_{l}^{m}v_{l}^{m}
and
Q_{l}^{m}
.
Now we define the elements of
\mathfrak{C}^{m}
as follows:

i)

The disk
\Delta^{m}\subseteq\Delta_{i-1}
is the closure of the component of
\Delta_{i-1}-T(P_{s+1}^{m})
which contains
T(P_{s}^{m})
, where
T(P_{l}^{m})
is the trace of
P_{l}^{m}
in
\rho
for
l\in[s+1]
.

ii)

The society
(G^{m},\Omega^{m})
is the
\Delta^{m}
-society in
\rho
.
Note that we have
V(\Omega^{m})\subseteq V(\Omega)\cup V(P^{m}_{s+1})
.

iii)

The even-faced cylindrical rendition
\rho^{m}
of
G^{m}
around
c^{m}
is a restriction of
\rho
to
\Delta^{m}
.

iv)

The vortex
c^{m}
is
C_{1}^{m}
-disk in
\rho^{m}
.

v)

The nest is
\mathcal{C}^{m}=\{C_{1}^{m},\ldots,C_{s}^{m}\}
.

vi)

To define the radial linkage
\mathcal{L}^{m}
, choose an end segment of
\mathcal{P}^{m}
in
(G,\Omega)
.
Let
Y^{m}
be the set of endpoints of
\mathcal{P}^{m}
contained in the chosen end segment, and let
\mathcal{L}^{m}
be the set of
Y^{m}
-
V(C_{1}^{m})
-subpaths of paths in
\mathcal{P}^{m}
.
Subsequently, let
X^{m}
be the set of endpoints of
\mathcal{L}^{m}
other than
Y^{m}
.
Observe that
\mathcal{L}^{m}
is also a
C_{1}
-
\Omega
-linkage.

Finally, we define new society configurations to be
\mathfrak{C}^{m}\coloneqq((G^{m},\Omega^{m}),\rho^{m},c^{m},\Delta^{m},\mathcal{C}^{m},\mathcal{L}^{m},X^{m},Y^{m})
.
Note that
\mathcal{L}^{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}
are disjoint.

Vortex refinement tree

Let
k
be a positive integer.
A
vortex refinement tree
is a tuple
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\mathfrak{B},\Lambda)
, where

i)

T
is a rooted tree such that each node has at most
2
children, whose nodes are marked as either “incomplete” or “completed”.
In addition, each node is marked as either “moving” or “staying”, where any node marked as moving has a parent that is either the root or has two children,

ii)

\mathfrak{B}
is a mapping assigning each node
v\in V(T)
to a society configuration, and

iii)

\Lambda
is a closed set in
\Delta
whose boundary intersects
\rho
only at the nodes.

Here,
\Lambda
will be the area where our linkages are allowed to pass through.

7.2
Proof of our Society Classification Theorem

We are now ready to prove the main theorem of this section.

Proof of
??
.

Let

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\coloneqq~

\displaystyle\lceil c_{\ref{thm:ktminormodel}}t\sqrt{\log 12t}\rceil

Building a vortex refinement tree:

k
-surface wall.

We iteratively apply
??
to each incomplete leaf node.
In each step, one of the following happens: i) we stop the iteration concluding
(S2)
or
(S7)
, ii) we add a new node as a child and mark it as completed and moving, iii) we add two new incomplete moving leaf nodes as children, or iv) we add one new incomplete staying leaf node as a unique child.

Now, suppose that

has at most
b^{\prime\prime}
leaf nodes, and there is an incomplete leaf node

.
Note that since each moving node has at most two children,

has

ancestors marked as moving.
Let
\mathfrak{B}(t_{i})=\mathfrak{S}_{i}\coloneqq((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{\mathsf{nest}^{\prime}(t,h,k)-(k+1)(d_{i}+2)}^{i}\},\mathcal{L}_{i},X_{i},Y_{i})
.
Suppose that the restriction of
\rho^{\prime}
to
G_{i}
contains at most
b^{\prime}
vortices.
We apply
??
to

to obtain one of the following results in time

.

i)

The society
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
has depth at most
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lem:separating_evenfaced_planar_transaction}}(\mathsf{nest}^{\prime}(t,h,k)-(k+1)d_{i},b^{\prime},2\mathsf{radial}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,h,k)+2\mathsf{nest}^{\prime}(t,h,k)+2,h,\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t^{\prime},k,2ph+p))

ii)

There is an odd minor model for the universal parity breaking grid

controlled by a mesh whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from

iii)

There is a separating even-faced planar transaction

of order

with

-strip society, such that

to

iv)

of order

in
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
.

We start by discussing the first and the third options listed above.
The second and the fourth option are addressed later on.
If we obtain the first option, we simply mark
t_{i}
as completed.

Now, suppose that we obtain the third option.
In this case, we divide the
(\mathsf{nest}^{\prime}(t,h,k)-(k+1)d_{i},\mathsf{radial}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,h,k))
-society configuration
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
by the transaction
\mathcal{P}_{i}
of order
2\mathsf{radial}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,h,k)+2\mathsf{nest}^{\prime}(t,h,k)+2
to obtain two new society configurations
\mathfrak{S}^{L}_{i}=((G^{L}_{i},\Omega^{L}_{i}),\rho^{L}_{i},c^{L}_{i},\Delta^{L}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{L},\mathcal{L}_{i}^{L},X^{L}_{i},Y_{i}^{L})
and
\mathfrak{S}^{R}_{i}=((G^{R}_{i},\Omega^{R}_{i}),\rho^{R}_{i},c^{R}_{i},\Delta^{R}_{i},\mathcal{C}^{R}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{R}_{i},X^{R}_{i},Y^{R}_{i})
.
As there is a linkage
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{m}
with
m\in\{L,R\}
for

, there is a block

in

Suppose that both

.
Here, we move in by

to make sure that there are enough cycles when constructing a parity

-surface wall, and there is a linkage between nests and the base cycles.
We update

Furthermore, for each

Lastly, let

.

Now consider the other case where

or

Note that only one of

Let

so that

Then we add an incomplete staying node

as a unique child of

where

.

Lastly, notice that whenever we obtain the third option to grow the vortex refinement tree, the number of vertices in the union of

for every leaf node

Therefore, the whole process will end before
|V(G)|
iteration, so we have

.

Building a linkage:

Before dealing with the second and fourth option, we explain how to build a linkage that connects the society configurations we found in each step to the original society configuration.
Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\mathfrak{B},\Lambda)
be a vortex refinement tree and let

be a set of leaf nodes.
For each node

, let

We traverse the vortex refinement tree starting from

toward the root to find a

-

-linkage

of order

-

•

-

We find

i)

If

.

ii)

If

is also a

Thus,

-

Therefore, we can apply

to

,

\mathcal{L}_{u}
to find

.

Observe that the second and the third condition of

follow from the fact that

results from

of the definition of dividing a society configuration.

We repeat the process all the way up to the root of

, to find a desired
(\bigcup_{i\in[\ell]}X_{t_{i}})
-
Y_{0}
-linkage.
Furthermore, since

, we can find such a linkage in time

.

Now, suppose that we obtain the second option of
??
.
Note that there is a
X_{i}
-

So, the odd minor model for

If we obtain the fourth option of

Building a surface wall:

Suppose that at some point in the construction of the vortex refinement tree, we have
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\mathfrak{B},\Lambda)
such that

Then either

Since it holds that

Then we build a parity-

.

For each

with

.
Let

be the set of vertices corresponds to
\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime}
via radial linkage which has
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Then similarly as before, we can find a
\bigcup_{i\in[h]}X_{i}^{\prime}
-
Y_{1}
-linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order

.
Then for each

X_{i}^{\prime}
-

-linkage of order

that is subset of
\mathcal{R}
, and naturally index the paths in this linkage as
P^{i}_{1},\ldots,P^{i}_{4h(2h+1)}
.
For each

, let
Z_{j}^{i}
be the minimal segment of

that contains the endpoints of
P^{i}_{4(j-1)(2h+1)+1},\ldots,P^{i}_{4j(2h+1)}
, and let

be the area bounded by the trace of

,
P^{i}_{4j(2h+1)}
,

\mathcal{C}_{i}
that contains

.
Then by the pigeonhole principle, for at least one of

,
D^{i}_{j}
does not contain all other

.
Fix such

for each

.
We can extend these paths by concatenating the paths in the
\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
and the paths in the

, we can find
\bigcup_{i\in[h]}X_{i}^{\mathfrak{D}}
-

-linkage such that for each

, it contains

-

-linkage of order

, which is orthogonal to both

and

With

for

, we can build a parity-

-surface-wall with signature

by using

Since the

we chose are not quasi-bipartite, we can add a parity breaking paths whose endpoints are on the innermost cycle of

.
This allows us to apply

to find an odd minor model of

If there is no active leaf node while we have

leaf nodes, we build a parity

-surface-wall with signature

for

similarly as above.
Furthermore, the whole process can be done in time
(f_{\ref{thm:ktminormodel}}(t)+\mathbf{poly}(h+s+p+k))|E(G)||V(G)|^{2}
.
∎

8
Local structure

Now that we have gone through the arduous task of adapting the Society Classification Theorem for our purposes, we need to also reprove the Local Structure Theorem in our setting.
This theorem first appears in
[
91
]
as Theorem 3.1, with Theorem 15.1 being the corresponding statement in
[
42
]
.
We will follow the proof in
[
42
]
fairly closely and thus not elaborate too much on its details, since those can be found in
[
42
]
.
Instead we will focus on proving that graphs which admit the type of local structure that can be found in our theorem indeed do have bounded odd cycle packing number.

Before we can state our first local structure theorem, we will need to introduce another concept relating to the way we represent our graph on a surface.
This definition is specific to the local structure theorem and we can forget about it again once we have determined that the resulting structure does not permit graphs with arbitrary odd cycle packing number.

These definitions differ slightly from those found in
[
42
]
as we desire some slightly different restrictions related to even-faced renditions and the fact that some of our vortices are allowed to not have a cross as long as they are non-bipartite in exchange.

Non-orientable, even landscapes.

Let
k,w\geq 4
be integers, let
G
be a graph, and let
\Sigma
be a surface of Euler-genus
g
.
Let
h
,
c^{o}
,
c^{e}
, and
b
be non-negative integers where
g=2h+c
and
c=c^{o}+c^{e}\neq 0
if and only if
\Sigma
is non-orientable.
Moreover, let
D\subseteq G
be a parity
k
-surface-wall with signature
(0,h,c^{o},c^{e},0,b)
, let
W\subseteq G-A
be a
w
-mesh in
G
, and let
\mathcal{T}_{D}
and
\mathcal{T}_{W}
be the tangles they respectively define.
Finally, let
A\subseteq V(G)\setminus V(D)
.

The tuple
\Lambda=(A,W,D,\rho)
is called a
non-orientable, even
\Sigma
-landscape
of
detail
k
and
oddness
c^{o}
if

L1

\rho
is a non-orientable, even-faced
\Sigma
-rendition of the large block of
\mathcal{T}_{W}
,

L2

D
and
W
are grounded in
\rho
,

L3

W
is flat in
\rho
,

L4

the disk bounded by the trace of the simple cycle of
D
in
\rho
avoids the traces of the other base cycles of
D
,

L5

the tangle
\mathcal{T}_{D}
is a truncation of the tangle
\mathcal{T}_{W}
,

L6

if
C
is a cycle from the nest of some vortex-segment of
D
, then the trace of
C
is a contractible closed curve in
\Sigma
,

L7

\rho
has exactly
b
vortices and there exists a bijection between the vortices
v
of
\rho
and the vortex segments
S_{v}
of
D
such that
v
is the unique vortex of
\rho
that is contained in the
v
-disk
\Delta_{C_{1}}
of the inner cycle of
S_{v}
, where
\Delta_{C_{1}}
avoids the trace of the simple cycle of
D
, and

L8

for every vortex
v
of
\rho
, the society induced by the outer cycle from the nest of the corresponding vortex segment is non-bipartite or has a cross.

We refer to the vortex segments as the
vortices
of
\Lambda
and call
A
the
apex set
.
The integer
b
is called the
breadth
of
\Lambda
and the
depth
of
\Lambda
is the depth of
\rho
.
We say that
\Lambda
is
centred
at the mesh
W
.

Non-orientable, even layouts.

Let
k\geq 4
,
l
,
d
,
b
,
r
, and
a
be non-negative integers and
\Sigma
be a surface.
We say that a graph
G
with a mesh
M
has a
non-orientable, even
(k,l)
-
(a,b,d,r)
-
\Sigma
-layout

centred at
M
if there exists a set
A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
a
and a submesh
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
such that there exists a non-orientable, even
\Sigma
-landscape
(A,M^{\prime},D,\rho)
of detail
k
, oddness
l
, breadth
b
, and depth
d
for
G
where every vortex of
\rho
has a linear decomposition of adhesion at most
d
, and
M^{\prime}
is a
w
-mesh with
w\geq a+b(2d+1)+6+r
.

8.1
A Local Structure Theorem

We will now present a theorem in the style of Theorem 15.1 in
[
42
]
.
Note that this is not the ultimate goal of this section, since we will proceed to show that this result further implies that the graphs we are interested in have bounded
OCP
.
We present this result separately due to its conceptual closeness to the aforementioned result from
[
42
]
and due to the sheer length and technical complexity of the proof of this result.

Recall that, given some
r
-mesh
M
and a separation
(A,B)
of order at most
r-1
we said that
X\in\{A,B\}
is the
majority side
of
(A,B)
if
X\setminus Y
, where
Y\in\{A,B\}\setminus\{X\}
, contains the vertices of both a vertical and a horizontal path of
M
.
Moreover, we observed that the orientation
\mathcal{T}_{M}
of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
is a tangle.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface and let
\rho
be a
\Sigma
-rendition of a graph
G
containing an
r
-mesh
M
.
We say that
\rho
is
M
-central
if there is no cell
c\in C(\rho)
such that
V(\sigma(c))
contains the majority side of a separation from
\mathcal{T}_{M}
.
Similarly, let
A\subseteq V(G)
,
|A|\leq r-1
, let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be a surface and
\rho^{\prime}
be a
\Sigma^{\prime}
-rendition of
G-A
.
Then we say that
\rho^{\prime}
is
(M-A)
-central
for
G
if no cell of
\rho^{\prime}
contains the majority side of a separation from
\mathcal{T}_{M}\cap\mathcal{S}_{r-|A|}
.

Theorem 8.1
.

There exist functions
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}},\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}},\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all positive integers
k
,
t
,
r
, and
t^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)
, every graph
G
, and every
\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,r,k)
-mesh
M\subseteq G
one of the following holds.

i)

There exists a set
X\subseteq G
, with
|X|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t^{\prime})
, the large block of
\mathcal{T}_{M}-X
is bipartite, where
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is the tangle associated with
M
,

ii)

G
has
\mathscr{H}_{t}
as an odd minor controlled by
M
, or

iii)

G
has a non-orientable, even
(k,l)
-
(\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t^{\prime}-3)(t^{\prime}-4)+t-1,\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k),r)
-
\Sigma
-layout
\Lambda
centred at
M
where
l<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t^{\prime}-3)(t^{\prime}-4)+t-1
, the surface
\Sigma
has genus less than
{t^{\prime}}^{2}
, and the
\Sigma
-rendition
\rho
of
\Lambda
is
(M-A)
-central where
A
is the apex set of
\Lambda
.

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\in&\ \mathbf{O}(t^{4}),\\
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\in&\ \mathbf{O}(t^{12}),\\
\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k),~\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)\in&\ \mathbf{O}\big((t+k)^{150}\big),\text{ and}\\
\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,r,k)\in&\ \mathbf{O}\big((t+k)^{161}+rt^{12}\big).\end{aligned}

There also exists an algorithm that, given
t
,
k
,
r
, a graph
G
, and a mesh
M
as above as input finds one of these outcomes in time
(f_{\ref{thm:ktminormodel}}(t)+\mathsf{poly}(k))|E(G)||V(G)|^{2}
.

Extending the surface.

A major part of the proof of the Local Structure Theorem consists of using the opportunity provided by the options in
??
that gives us crosscap and handle transactions to grow the surface we are embedding our graph in.
To accomplish this we extract two results from
[
60
]
(see Lemma 10.2 and Lemma 10.5), one for the crosscap case and the other for handles.
A more thorough, illustrated explanation of the intuition behind these two statements is given in
[
42
]
.
We adopt the minor changes from
[
42
]
to these statements which involve only considering a radial linkage instead of a linkage from
\Omega
to the vortex.
This comes at a very minor cost in the number of cycles we lose.
More importantly, we note that by applying
??
into the proof of Lemma 10.2 and Lemma 10.5 whenever
??
is applied (see Lemma 5.15 from
[
60
]
), we can also ensure additional properties for the resulting rendition.
For the crosscap case, this tells us that the new rendition is even-faced and non-orientable.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
s
and
p
be non-negative integers.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with an even-faced, cylindrical rendition
\rho_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s+9}\}
around the vortex
c_{0}
.
Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be disjoint segments of
\Omega
such that there exist

•

a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
starting in
X_{1}
, and

•

an even-faced, flat crosscap transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order at least
p+2s+7
with all endpoints in
X_{2}
and disjoint from
\mathcal{R}
.

Let
\Sigma^{*}
be a surface, homeomorphic to the projective plane minus an open disk, which is obtained from
\Delta
by adding a crosscap to the interior of
c_{0}
.

Then there exists a crosscap transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
, consisting of the middle
p
paths of
\mathcal{P}
, and a non-orientable, even-faced rendition
\rho_{1}
of
(G,\Omega)
in
\Sigma^{*}
(around the
\bigcup\mathcal{C}
) with a unique vortex
c_{1}
and the following hold:

i)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is disjoint from
\sigma(c_{1})
,

ii)

the vortex society of
c_{1}
in
\rho_{1}
has an even-faced, cylindrical rendition
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
with a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{s}^{\prime}\}
around the unique vortex
c_{1}^{\prime}
,

iii)

every element of
\mathcal{R}
has an endpoint in
V(\sigma_{\rho_{1}^{\prime}}(c_{1}^{\prime}))
,

iv)

\mathcal{R}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and for every
i\in[s]
and every
R\in\mathcal{R}
,
C_{i}^{\prime}\cap R=C_{i+8}\cap R
. Moreover,

v)

let
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell}\}
.
For each
i\in[\ell]
let
x_{i}
be the endpoint of
R_{i}
in
X_{1}
, and let
y_{i}
be the last vertex of
R_{i}
on

when traversing along
R_{i}
starting from
x_{i}
; if
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\ell}
appear in
\Omega
in the order listed, then
y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{\ell}
appear on
\mathsf{bd}(c_{1})
in the order listed.

Finally, let
\Delta^{\prime}
be the open disk bounded by the trace of
C_{s+8}
in
\rho_{0}
.
Then
\rho_{0}
restricted to
\Delta\setminus\Delta^{\prime}
is equal to
\rho_{1}
restricted to
\Delta\setminus\Delta^{\prime}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that computes this outcome in time
\mathbf{poly}(sp|\mathcal{R}|)|V(G)|
.

The case in which we try to integrate a handle is more complex.
As we can see in
??
, either of the two planar transactions making up the handle transaction may cause odd cycles to appear.
For us, if this happens for at least one of the two planar transactions, this is a way to build one of our obstructions.
Thus we are satisfied with the following version of the statement.

.

Helpful tools for the proof.

Another smattering of tools is distributed throughout different parts of
[
42
]
.
First, we will need to connect two orthogonal radial linkages to build a new orthogonal radial linkage.

Proposition 8.5

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
42
]
)
.

Let
s,r,k,\ell
be positive integers with
s\geq r+3
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
\Sigma
-rendition
\rho
and a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
each be radial linkages of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that both are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and let
I=[\ell,k]\subseteq[2,s]
be an interval with
|I|=r+2
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[s]\setminus I\}
with endpoints on
C_{1}
,

ii)

H_{\ell}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{L}
, where
H_{\ell}
is the inner graph of
C_{\ell}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(C_{1})
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
on
V(C_{1})
, and

iii)

H_{k}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{k}\cap\mathcal{R}
, where
H_{k}
is the outer graph of
C_{k}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(\Omega)
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{R}
on
V(\Omega)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
.

Another important ingredient in our proof will be the ability to build layouts via finding surface walls and
K_{t}
-minors within them.
For this purpose we first introduce so-called “surface configurations”.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition and a nest
\mathcal{C}
around the vortex
c_{0}
.
Further let
\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
be a set of transactions on
(G,\Omega)
as well as
\mathcal{R}
be a radial linkage such that

•

V(\mathcal{P}_{i})\cap V(\mathcal{P}_{j})=\emptyset
for all
i\neq j\in[\ell]
as well as
V(\mathcal{R})\cap V(\mathcal{P}_{i})=\emptyset
for all
i\in[\ell]
,

•

for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{R}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
, and

•

there exist pairwise disjoint segments
I_{1},J_{1},I_{2},J_{2},\dots,I_{\ell},J_{\ell},R
of
\Omega
such that these segments appear on
\Omega
in the order listed, for each
i\in[\ell]
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a
V(I_{i})
-
V(J_{i})
-linkage, and each path from
\mathcal{R}
has one endpoint in
V(R)
.

We call the tuple
(G,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
a
surface configuration
of
(G,\Omega)
and the sequence
(I_{1},J_{1},I_{2},J_{2},\dots,I_{\ell},J_{\ell},R)
the
signature
of
(G,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{P})
.
Finally we say that
(s,r,p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell})
is the
strength
of
(G,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{P})
if
|\mathcal{C}|=s
,
|\mathcal{R}|=r
and
|\mathcal{P}_{i}|=p_{i}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

The following core observation on surface configurations stems from
[
42
]
as well.

Observation 8.6
.

Let
k\geq 3
,
\ell
,
h
, and
c
be non-negative integers with
\ell=h+c
.
Moreover, let
s\geq k
,
r\geq 4k
,
p_{i}\geq 4k
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Then, for every surface configuration
(G,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{P})
of strength
(s,r,p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell})
with
h
handle-transactions and
c
crosscap-transactions,
G
contains a
k
-surface-wall
W
with
h
handles and
k
crosscaps as a subgraph such that the base cycles of
W
are cycles from
\mathcal{C}
and the vertical paths of the
k
-wall-segment of
W
are subpaths of the paths from
\mathcal{R}
.

We will also need the following result that allows us to extract minors from surface walls representing surfaces of sufficient genus.

Proposition 8.7

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
42
]
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}
such that for all non-negative integers
c,h,t
, every graph
H
that embeds in a surface homeomorphic to the surface obtained from the sphere by adding
h
handles and
c
crosscaps is a minor of the extended
\big(c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}g^{4}(|V(H)|+g)^{2}\big)
-surface wall
W
with
h
handles,
c
crosscaps, and 0 vortices, where
g=2h+c
.
Moreover, if
H
is a complete graph, then there exists an
H
-minor model controlled by
W
.

In particular,
K_{t}
is a minor for every extended
k
-surface-wall with
h
handles and
c
crosscaps where
2h+c=t^{2}
and
k\in\mathbf{\Omega}(t^{12})
.

The proof of the Local Structure Theorem.

We begin by providing more accurate estimates for the functions we will use throughout our proofs.
This also serves to justify the bounds we later claim in the statement in
??
.

We begin by establishing a few auxiliary functions that track certain constructions in our proof.
The parameter
g
here is used to remember how many times we have increased the genus of our surface so far.

\displaystyle\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle\lceil\mathsf{c}_{\ref{thm:ktminormodel}}(12t^{2}+6t)\sqrt{\log 144t^{2}+72t}\rceil

\displaystyle\mathsf{radial}(g,t,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle(g+2)(8k+8c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+4+\mathsf{radial}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t),t,k)+1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{nest}(g,t,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle(g+2)\big(8k+8c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+\mathsf{radial}(g-1,t,k)+14+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,4(k+c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+1))

\displaystyle+\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t)\big)+\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t),t,4(k+c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+1))+\nicefrac{{4k}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{transaction}(g,t,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle 4k+4c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+24+4\mathsf{nest}(g-1,t,k)+2\mathsf{radial}(g-1,t,k)

For these functions, based on the bounds in
[
42
]
, we can give the following estimates:

\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\in\mathbf{O}\in(t^{4})
,
\mathsf{radial}(g,t,k)\in\mathbf{O}(gt^{12}+gkt^{8})
,
\mathsf{nest}(g,t,k),\mathsf{transaction}(g,t,k)\in\mathbf{O}(g^{2}t^{12}+kg^{2}t^{8}+t^{36}+kt^{32})
.
With these values fixed, the remaining functions of
??
are as follows.

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle\max(2\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t),8\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)^{3})

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle t^{2}\cdot\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,t,4(k+c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+1),\mathsf{transaction}(t^{2},t,k))+16t^{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t),t,4(k+c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+1),\mathsf{transaction}(t^{2},t,k))

\displaystyle\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,r,k)\coloneqq~

\displaystyle 100\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)^{3}\Big(\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)+2\mathsf{nest}(t^{2},t,k)+2\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)+11+r

\displaystyle+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)(2\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:evensocietyclassification}}(t,4(k+c_{\ref{prop:universal-surface-walls}}t^{12}+1),\mathsf{transaction}(t^{2},t,k))+1)\Big)

This means we can bound the above functions as follows:

\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)\in\mathbf{O}(t^{12})
,
\mathsf{apex}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)\in\mathbf{O}((t+k)^{150})
,
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,k)\in\mathbf{O}((t+k)^{141})
, and
\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,r,k)\in\mathbf{O}((t+k)^{161}+rt^{12})
.

We note that in many aspects our proof closely follows the core proof of the local structure theorem in
[
42
]
(see the proof of Theorem 15.4).
Our main adjustments are further case distinctions when applying
??
,
??
, and other results.

Proof of
??
.

We let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{clique}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)
and let
M
be a
\mathsf{mesh}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t,r,k)
-mesh in
G
.
We first apply
??
to
M
using the values
t^{\prime}
and
r
.
In
\mathbf{O}(f_{\ref{thm:ktminormodel}}(t^{\prime})|V(G)|^{\omega_{\ref{def:matrixmultconstant}}})
-time, the first three options yields either a universal parity breaking grid of order
3t
controlled by
M
, an odd minor model
\varphi
of
K_{12t^{2}+6t}
controlled by
M
, a set
X\subseteq V(G)
with
|X|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{bip}}_{\ref{thm:strongest_localstructure}}(t)
, such that the large block of
\mathcal{T}_{M}-A
is bipartite, or a set
A_{0}
with
|A_{0}|<16{t^{\prime}}^{3}
and an even-faced
r^{\prime}
-submesh
M^{\prime}
of
M
that is disjoint from
A_{0}
and flat in
G-A_{0}
.
In the first and second case, we can simply construct a model of
\mathscr{H}_{t}
that is controlled by
M
by reducing both cases first to the universal parity breaking grid of order
3t
and then applying
??
to find our desired parity handle of order
t
.
The third case in turn immediately satisfies our statement.

Thus we may assume that we arrive in the last option of
??
, yielding a set
A_{0}
and an even-faced
r^{\prime}
-submesh
M^{\prime}
of
M
that is disjoint from
A_{0}
and flat in
G-A_{0}
, with

\displaystyle r^{\prime}\geq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{100{t^{\prime}}^{3}}(\mathsf{mesh}(t^{\prime},k,r)-2t^{\prime}-2)
